import { kea, resetContext, getContext } from '../../src'

import React, { Component } from 'react'
import { render, screen, act } from '@testing-library/react'

class SampleComponent extends Component {
  render() {
    const { id, name, capitalizedName, upperCaseName } = this.props
    const { updateName } = this.actions

    return (
      <div data-testid={`sample-${id}`}>
        <div data-testid="id">{id}</div>
        <div data-testid="name">{name}</div>
        <div data-testid="capitalizedName">{capitalizedName}</div>
        <div data-testid="upperCaseName">{upperCaseName}</div>
        <div data-testid="updateName" onClick={updateName}>
          updateName
        </div>
      </div>
    )
  }
}

class ActionComponent extends Component {
  render() {
    return (
      <div>
        <div data-testid="actions">{Object.keys(this.actions).sort().join(',')}</div>
        <div data-testid="props">{Object.keys(this.props).sort().join(',')}</div>
        <div data-testid="name">{this.props.name}</div>
      </div>
    )
  }
}
describe('logic component', () => {
  beforeEach(() => {
    resetContext()
  })

  test('singletons connect to react components', () => {
    const { store } = getContext()

    const singletonLogic = kea({
      path: () => ['scenes', 'something'],
      actions: () => ({
        updateName: (name) => ({ name }),
      }),
      reducers: ({ actions }) => ({
        name: [
          'chirpy',
          {
            [actions.updateName]: (state, payload) => payload.name,
          },
        ],
      }),
      selectors: ({ selectors }) => ({
        upperCaseName: [
          () => [selectors.capitalizedName],
          (capitalizedName) => {
            return capitalizedName.toUpperCase()
          },
        ],
        capitalizedName: [
          () => [selectors.name],
          (name) => {
            return name
              .trim()
              .split(' ')
              .map((k) => `${k.charAt(0).toUpperCase()}${k.slice(1).toLowerCase()}`)
              .join(' ')
          },
        ],
      }),
    })

    const ConnectedComponent = singletonLogic(SampleComponent)

    render(<ConnectedComponent id={12} />)

    expect(screen.getByTestId('id')).toHaveTextContent('12')
    expect(screen.getByTestId('name')).toHaveTextContent('chirpy')
    expect(screen.getByTestId('capitalizedName')).toHaveTextContent('Chirpy')
    expect(screen.getByTestId('upperCaseName')).toHaveTextContent('CHIRPY')

    expect(store.getState()).toEqual({ kea: {}, scenes: { something: { name: 'chirpy' } } })

    act(() => singletonLogic.actions.updateName('somename'))

    expect(store.getState()).toEqual({ kea: {}, scenes: { something: { name: 'somename' } } })

STEP 20 SOURCE agent
REASONING_CONTENT
No existing implementation. This is a substantial feature. Let me understand the task deeply and design.

The task: Introduce an "Atomic Signal Selector Engine" to Kea that enables fine-grained reactivity. It's an opt-in feature via `resetContext({
atomicSelectors: true })`, defaults false.

Requirements:
1. **Dependency Tracking**: Track selector dependencies at exact leaf level (e.g. `user.name`). Accessing `user.name` must NOT cause re-evaluation
when `user.age` changes. Dependencies exposed via `logic.selectorHealth()`. Dependencies list leaf paths read (e.g. `user.name`), not parent nodes.
Stable identity (e.g., combining `logic.pathString` and selector's local name) that persists through build-time function wrapping.

2. **Support for Collections**: fine-grained access in Maps/Sets/Arrays. For Map key access: `<reducer>.map:<key>`; Set membership:
`<reducer>.set:<value>`; Array indices: `<reducer>.<index>`.

3. **Propagation**: multi-level selector chains where updates propagate only to affected selectors. If selector's inputs haven't changed, it should
not re-evaluate.

4. **Atomic Updates**: Multiple dependency changes within a single action must trigger exactly one re-evaluation of a dependent selector.

5. **Circular Safety**: Detect and prevent circular dependency loops during logic mounting/building phase. Throw error containing exact string `[KEA]
Circular dependency detected`.

6. **Compatibility**: All baseline Kea behaviors (lifecycle, mounting order) unchanged. The new engine intercepts core lifecycle hooks; valid
implementations must ensure standard plugin event ordering (e.g., `afterMount`) is not disrupted.

7. **React Integration**: Components re-render only when their accessed state or derived selectors change. Unrelated state updates must not trigger
re-renders.

Health API:
- When `atomicSelectors` is true, expose `logic.selectorHealth()` as a function. When disabled, `logic.selectorHealth` must be `undefined`.
- Returns `{ selectors: { [name]: { dependencies: string[], dependents: string[], evaluations: number, dirtyCause: string | null } },
topologicalOrder: string[] }`
- dependencies: relative paths (e.g. `user.name`) or local selector names.
- dependents: local names of selectors that depend on this one.
- evaluations: total number of times selector's compute function invoked.
- dirtyCause: identifier that triggered most recent invalidation. `selector:<localName>` when caused by another selector, raw leaf paths when caused
by state change. Local to logic (no pathString prefix).
- topologicalOrder: array of selector names sorted by evaluation order in dependency graph.

This is a huge feature. Essentially we need to replace reselect's behavior with a custom reactive engine when enabled. Let's think about how Kea
currently works:

- Kea builds `logic.selectors[name]` functions using reselect's `createSelector(args, func, { memoizeOptions })`.
- Each selector is a function `(state, props) => ...`.
- `logic.selector` is the root selector: `(state) => pathSelector(logic.path, state)`.
- Reducer selectors are created via `createSelector(logic.selector!, (state) => state[key])`.
- `useSelector` in React uses `useSyncExternalStore` with `getContext().store.subscribe` and a snapshot function that calls the selector. So currently
any store change re-runs the snapshot function (reselect memoization means the selector only recomputes when inputs changed). React's
`useSyncExternalStore` calls getSnapshot after each store notification; getSnapshot returns a value; React only re-renders if value changed by
Object.is. So actually currently React already avoids re-renders on unrelated state changes (as long as selector memoization works). But the
requirement for atomic selectors is finer: if a selector accesses `user.name`, an update to `user.age` should not cause re-evaluation. Reselect's
default memoization only tracks the input selector results — if `user` object changes, `selectors.user` returns a new object (since reducer creates
new object), and any derived selector depending on `user` will re-run even if only `age` changed. The atomic engine tracks at leaf level so
`user.name` doesn't re-evaluate when `user.age` changes.

So we need to:
- Track exactly which paths are read during selector execution (leaf-level).
- Subscribe to those paths, so when a change occurs only to read paths, invalidate accordingly.
- For React, `useSelector` must only re-render when the selected value changes (which already works), but also avoid calling compute when unrelated
changes happen. Actually the requirement says "Components must re-render only when their accessed state or derived selectors change. Unrelated state
updates must not trigger re-renders." The current reselect approach already satisfies the re-render part because useSyncExternalStore compares
snapshots. But it may not satisfy "not trigger re-evaluation" — the requirement says in React integration, components re-render only when accessed
state changes. This is satisfied by reselect too. But the evaluation count should be tracked and atomic behavior.

Let me think about how to implement this cleanly in Kea.

Approach: When `atomicSelectors` is enabled, we replace the selector creation path in `src/core/selectors.ts` and `src/core/reducers.ts` with our
atomic engine. The atomic engine:

- Each selector is wrapped so that while it executes, we record dependencies at leaf-level by wrapping the state tree (or at least the parts accessed)
with proxies that track property access. We can use a Proxy that recursively returns proxies for nested objects, and for arrays/maps/sets, intercepts
methods/indices.
- Dependencies are relative to the logic root (the state object returned by `logic.selector`). E.g. if the logic root reducer has `user` and `list`,
then `user.name` is relative path from logic root. So the atomic engine wraps the result of `logic.selector(state)`? Actually selector functions
receive the full Redux state, and they first call `logic.selector(state)` to get logic-local state. We can instrument at the point of reading.

Let me think about the architecture in detail.

Kea selectors:
1. Root selector `logic.selector = (state) => pathSelector(logic.path, state)` - gets the logic's slice.
2. Reducer selectors: `createSelector(logic.selector, (state) => state[key])` - takes the root slice and returns `state[key]`.
3. User selectors: `createSelector(args..., func)` where args are `logic.selectors[...]` functions and prop selectors, and func is the compute.

In atomic mode, we can create a custom `createSelector` replacement that builds an "atomic selector" instead of reselect's. Actually the selector
functions are used by React `useSelector` and by `logic.values`. We need each `logic.selectors[name]` to be a function `(state, props) => value` that:
- Tracks dependencies while running (via proxied state).
- Registers itself as dependent of other selectors it calls (selector-level dependencies).
- On state updates, the engine knows which leaf paths changed (from Redux state diff) and invalidates affected selectors.
- Memoizes results: if no invalidated dependency, returns cached result.
- Exposes evaluation count and dirtyCause.

How to track state changes at leaf level? When Redux dispatches an action, the new state is computed. We can compare previous vs new state to compute
a set of changed paths. Then for each selector, check if any of its dependencies intersect changed paths; if so mark dirty. For selectors that depend
on other selectors, mark dirty when the input selector is dirty (or when its result changes — but to avoid evaluating unnecessarily, we want
propagation only to affected selectors).

But wait — selector-level dependencies: if selector B depends on selector A, and A's dependency `user.age` changed but A doesn't read `user.name`, A
is not dirty, so B is not dirty. That's the granularity.

How do we compute changed paths from state diff? We can recursively diff old state and new state. That could be expensive but fine for opt-in.
However, when `user.age` changes, we need to know the path `user.age` changed. For Maps/Sets/Arrays, diffing needs special handling. Actually diffing
maps/sets/arrays deeply:
- Arrays: compare by index; if element at index i changed, path `list.i`.
- Maps: compare keys; if map.get('a') changed, path `data.map:a`; if key added/removed, path `data.map:a` (as membership) — task says "for Map key
access, `<reducer>.map:<key>`". So reading `data.get('a')` records dependency `data.map:a`. If that key's value changed or key added/removed, that
dependency is considered changed.
- Sets: `data.has('a')` records `data.set:a`. Membership changed when set membership changes.

Also for objects: if nested object reference changes, we should still diff recursively to identify leaf changes. But how deep? We need to know which
leaf changed. The reducer state tree can be large. Diffing the whole tree on every dispatch is O(state size) which is not ideal but acceptable for
this feature? Could be very expensive. Alternative: track dirty paths by wrapping reducer output. But Redux doesn't tell us what changed. The simplest
robust approach: on each action, walk both old and new state recursively, compare references, and record leaf paths that changed. But if an object
identity changes, we recurse into it to find which leaf changed. For large objects this is expensive. But this is an opt-in advanced feature; still
should be reasonable.

Actually, there's a subtlety: if `user.name` changes, redux reducer will create a new `user` object and new root state object. Diffing: root changed,
recurse: `user` changed, recurse: `name` changed -> record `user.name`. Good. If `user` object is replaced entirely with a new object with same `name`
but different `age`, diffing recursively would record `user.age` (and maybe `user.name` if name differs). Good.

But we also need to consider that `user.name` path is relative to the logic root. A given logic's selector accesses state via `logic.selector(state)`
-> path `scenes.something.user`. But dependencies in health are relative paths like `user.name`. So the atomic engine should record dependencies
relative to the root of the logic's slice. This is easiest if we instrument at the point after `logic.selector` returns the slice: i.e., wrap the
slice in a proxy that records `user.name` accesses. But user selector functions can also directly read global state? Typically they only use
`logic.selectors` and the props. And reducer selectors read from `logic.selector(state)`.

Let me think of a clean implementation:

- Define an `AtomicSelectorEngine` per logic, stored on `logic.atomicSelectorEngine` (or in a context WeakMap keyed by logic). It has:
  - `selectors: Map<name, AtomicSelector>` containing metadata: dependencies (Set of strings), dependents (Set of names), evaluations, dirtyCause,
cached result, dirty flag, compute function, args.
  - `rootSelector` for the logic.
  - `topologicalOrder`.
  - `changedPaths` (per action).

- Each `logic.selectors[name]` becomes an atomic selector function: `(state, props) => engine.runSelector(name, state, props)`.

- Running a selector:
  - If not dirty and cached, return cached result (increment nothing).
  - If dirty or no cache: run its compute function while tracking dependencies:

(after ensuring it's up-to-date). We record `selector:otherName` as a dependency. Actually dependencies array should list "local selector names" for

selectors. But we can wrap each input selector so when invoked during the input-collection phase (`input(logic.selectors, propSelectors)`), we know

selectors.bookId, (_, props) => props.extra]`; `args = input(logic.selectors, propSelectors)`. Then `builtSelectors[key] = createSelector(args,
func)`.

So for atomic mode, we can intercept: when the user's `input` function references `selectors.books`, we need to know that this is a selector
dependency. We can wrap `logic.selectors` in a proxy that returns an atomic selector wrapper function for each name. Then when `createSelector`-like
code calls those wrappers, we can record selector deps.

Let me design the atomic selector wrapper:
- `logic.selectors[name]` is itself the atomic wrapper `(state, props) => value`. When called from `useSelector` or values, it runs with dependency
tracking.
- For a user selector, we need to build it as: given args (array of functions) and compute func. We create a function `atomicSelector(state, props)`
that:
  1. Checks if dirty (or first run). If not dirty, return cached.
  2. Clears per-run dependency tracking; begins "capture" mode.
  3. Evaluates each arg selector: `args[i](state, props)`; if the arg is one of `logic.selectors[other]` wrappers, that wrapper records a dependency
on `other` (when called during capture) and returns other's current value (ensuring other is computed first, updating its deps).
  4. If arg is a plain function like `(_, props) => props.extra` (prop selector), calling it reads props, which we don't track (props changes handled
by propsChanged/rebuild).
  5. Calls `func(...argResults)`.
  6. After compute, commits the captured dependencies to the selector metadata: dependencies = selector deps + leaf deps captured via the state proxy.
  7. increments evaluations, sets cached result, dirty=false.
  8. returns result.

But the tricky part: the compute function `func` is what actually reads state? No — in reselect, `func` only receives the already-computed arg
results, not state directly. So leaf-level state reads happen inside the **input selector functions** (e.g., `selectors.books` returns the books
reducer's value; the `book` selector's func does `books[bookId]` on the **value**, not on the state). Wait, `func` can also do `books[bookId]` on a

records `books.<index>` if `books` is an array/map/set, or `books` if it's an object and index is a property... Actually the task example: "for Array

So the dependency capture needs to wrap:

Actually let's reconsider: In a selector chain, `book` selector depends on `books` and `bookId`. Its func does `books[bookId]`. The `books` value is a
plain object. If we wrap the value returned by the `books` selector in a tracking proxy, then when `func` does `books[bookId]`, it would record

the task says "Dependencies list the leaf paths read (e.g. user.name), not parent nodes." For an object, reading `books[bookId]` means reading the

`books.1` changes, selector is invalidated.

But wait: if `book` selector's dependency is `books.1` and `bookId` changes to 2, it re-evaluates because `selector:bookId` changed. It now reads
`books.2`, dependency set updates to `books.2`. Good.

So we need to wrap object values returned by selectors and state reads with proxies that record the full leaf path. This is essentially a deep proxy
over everything returned by selectors.

However, there's a subtlety with dependencies list requirement: "Dependencies list the leaf paths read (e.g. `user.name`), not parent nodes." For
reducer selectors like `books`, the selector just does `state.books` (reads the books reducer slice). That's a "leaf" at the root level: `books`.
That's fine. For `book` selector, the dependency on `books.1` is a leaf.

Now how does the engine know which paths changed? We diff the state tree to get changed leaf paths. Let's formalize:

- Every selector has a set of `stateDeps` (leaf paths relative to logic root) and `selectorDeps` (local names).
- On each Redux dispatch (after store update), the engine gets notified. It diffs oldState vs newState, producing a global set of changed paths
**relative to the root of the store** (e.g. `scenes.something.user.name`). For each mounted logic, it can map to its local paths by stripping the
logic path prefix. Or we can diff only the logic slices? Since we need to know all changed paths for all logic, easiest is to diff whole state. But
diffing whole state on every dispatch is expensive.

Alternative: Since we control the reducer, we can intercept at the logic-slice level. But multiple logic in different slices — each has own slice. The
root state changes propagate through combineKeaReducers, which knows which keys changed at each level. We could instrument `combineKeaReducers` to
record which leaf paths changed. That's a more efficient approach but invasive.

Simplest for this task: after each dispatch, run a function `notifyAtomicSelectors(previousState, nextState)` that diffs recursively and marks
affected selectors. Let's think about correctness and performance for tests. The test suite is small, so O(n) diff is fine. But if users have huge
stores, this could be slow. Still, opt-in feature. We could optimize by only diffing the parts of state that changed at the top level (using reference
equality to skip unchanged subtrees), and only tracking paths that any selector depends on. Let's implement a reasonable diff: recursively compare; if
old === new, skip. If both plain objects/arrays and at least one selector depends on something under that path, recurse; otherwise skip. Actually
simpler: recursively compare entire changed subtree and record all leaf differences. Since reference equality skips most unchanged subtrees, only the
changed branches are traversed. On a typical action that updates one field, the recursion depth is path length and breadth is small. That's
acceptable.

But there is a nuance: If a selector reads `user` (whole object) vs `user.name`, and the user object is replaced with a new object with same `name`
but changed `age`, diffing will record `user.age`. If the selector depends on `user.name`, it won't be invalidated. Good. If it depends on `user`
(parent), it should be invalidated. Our diff records leaf `user.age`, not `user`. So how does a selector that reads `user` (whole object) get
invalidated when `user.age` changes? Let's think: `s.user` read at the state proxy level — when a selector does `logic.selectors.user` (reducer
selector), that's a **selector dependency** on `user`, not a state leaf read. The `user` reducer selector reads `state.user` and returns the object.
Its own dependency is `user` (root leaf). When `user.age` changes, the reducer selector `user`'s state dependency is `user`, and changed paths include
`user.age`. Does `user` (as a dependency) match `user.age`? We need a matching rule: a selector depending on path `user` is invalidated by changes at
`user` OR any descendant (`user.*`). A selector depending on `user.name` is invalidated only by `user.name` and descendants of `user.name` (none), not
by `user.age`. So matching: change path `p` invalidates dependency `d` if `d === p` or `d` is a prefix of `p` (i.e., `p.startsWith(d + '.')` or d is
array index prefix). So `d='user'` invalidated by `user.age`; `d='user.name'` not.

So if a selector reads the whole `user` object from state (`state.user`), its dep is `user` and it will be invalidated by any change under user. But
the requirement "accessing `user.name` must NOT cause re-evaluation when `user.age` changes" means when a selector explicitly reads `user.name` (via
compute or via a selector that returns `user.name`), the dep is `user.name`. If it reads `user` object as a whole (e.g. `const user =
selectors.user`), then any change invalidates. That's semantically correct.

Now, to track when a selector reads `user.name` from the state proxy: if a selector does `logic.selectors.user(state, props)` and then `user.name`,
the `user` reducer selector returns a proxy-wrapped object; accessing `.name` on it records `user.name`. So the state dep is `user.name`, not `user`.
But the reducer selector `user` itself has dep `user` (it read `state.user`). However downstream selector only records `user.name` from the proxy.
Good — the parent selector's `user` dep doesn't matter for downstream invalidation; downstream is invalidated via its own leaf dep `user.name` or via
selector dep `user` when `user` selector's output changes. Wait, downstream selector's `dependencies` would be: `['user.name']` (from proxy) AND maybe
`['selector:user']`? Let's think: The downstream selector invokes `logic.selectors.user(state, props)` as an input arg. Our wrapper should record
`selector:user` as a dependency too. Then invalidation can happen either via `selector:user` or via `user.name`. If `user.age` changes, the `user`
selector output changes (because its dep `user` is invalidated -> new object). That would mark the `user` selector dirty. Then downstream's
`selector:user` dep would also be marked dirty, causing downstream re-evaluation even though it only reads `user.name`. That defeats fine-grained
granularity!

So we should NOT record selector dependencies for reducer selectors? Hmm. Let's think about what the intended behavior is.

The core idea: if a selector reads `user.name`, and `user.age` changes, the user reducer's returned object changes (new object), but the `.name`
property value is the same. We want the downstream selector NOT to re-evaluate. So we need propagation based on **actual values at the leaves** rather
than "input selector output object changed".

So the propagation model should be: a selector B depends on selector A. B should re-evaluate only if the **parts of A's output that B reads** change.
If B reads `A.name`, and A's output changes only in `.age`, B should not re-evaluate.

How to achieve this without B reading A's raw state? Since B's compute reads from A's returned value (possibly via proxy), we can record leaf
dependencies relative to the logic root: `user.name` (where `user` is the name of the A selector). When the user reducer returns a new object, the
engine can diff **A's old and new output** and derive which leaf paths changed (`user.age`), then invalidate B only if `user.name` is in the changed
leaf set. This works if we track dependencies of B as leaf paths into A's output (e.g. `user.name`) rather than `selector:user`.

But the health API says dependencies can be "local selector names" too. So there are selector-level dependencies in the graph (for topological
ordering and dependents). Maybe both are tracked: `dependencies` includes both leaf paths and selector names? Let's re-read: "`dependencies`: Array of
**relative** paths (e.g. `user.name`) or local selector names." So a selector may list both. E.g. `book: dependencies: ['selector:bookId', 'books.1']`
or maybe just `['bookId', 'books.1']`? It says "or local selector names", not necessarily prefixed with `selector:`. But `dirtyCause` uses
`selector:<localName>` for selector-caused. So dependencies likely include names like `bookId` (local selector name) and paths like `books.1`.

But if we propagate via `selector:bookId`, then when `bookId` changes, B invalidates. If `bookId` doesn't change but `books.1` changes, B should
invalidate via leaf dep. If `books.2` changes, B should not. So propagation can use both selector deps and leaf deps.

For the `user.age` case: B's dependencies include `selector:user` AND `user.name`? If we include `selector:user`, then when user's output object
changes (new reference), `selector:user` is considered changed and B re-evaluates. That's wrong. So maybe we should NOT mark `selector:user` as
changed unless the **value B reads** changed. But the health API wants dependents relationships. Let's think differently.

Maybe the engine should treat selector dependencies specially: when selector A re-evaluates and produces a new output, it computes a "changed leaf
set" by diffing A's old output vs new output (relative to the selector name, e.g. `user.age`, `user.name`). Then it propagates to dependents B: B is
invalidated if B's dependencies intersect A's changed leaf set OR B has a dependency on A's selector name... but the latter would cause spurious
invalidation.

Let's reconsider: What if B's dependency on A is represented in the metadata as a leaf-path dependency into A's output (e.g., `user.name`), and the
`dependencies` array shows `user.name`? Then where do "local selector names" appear? For a selector that passes through another selector's whole value
without reading a specific leaf, e.g. `double: [(s) => [s.count], (count) => count * 2]` — the `count` value is a number (primitive). The dependency
would be `count` (the local selector name / root leaf). For `book: [(s) => [s.books, s.bookId], ...]` reading `books[bookId]`, dep could be `books.1`
and `bookId` (selector name). Hmm.

Actually maybe the cleanest model:
- Each selector's dependencies are the **leaf paths** within the logic's state slice that it reads, where "leaf" includes:
  - If it reads a selector's whole primitive output, dep is the selector name (e.g., `bookId`).
  - If it reads into an object/array/map/set returned by a selector, dep is `selectorName.path` (e.g., `user.name`, `list.0`, `data.map:a`).
- Additionally, we maintain a separate **dependents graph** for propagation/topological ordering (each selector knows which selectors depend on it),
but for invalidation we only use the leaf paths.

How to know that B reads `user.name`? When B's compute calls `logic.selectors.user(state, props)` and then accesses `.name` on the result, the
returned value is a proxy that records `user.name` (prefixed with the selector name `user`). If B just uses the whole primitive `bookId`, the returned
value is a primitive so no property access; the dependency would be `bookId` (the selector name) recorded when B calls the selector.

So:
- When a selector B calls selector A's wrapper as an input arg, we record a provisional dependency on selector A.
- If B accesses properties of A's returned value via the proxy, those property accesses refine the dependency to leaf paths (e.g., `A.prop`), possibly
replacing the provisional selector dep? Or we keep both? For invalidation we should use only leaf-level. But for health `dependencies` we probably
want to show what's actually read. The requirement says "Dependencies list the leaf paths read (e.g. user.name), not parent nodes." So for B reading
`user.name`, dependencies should be `['user.name']` (leaf), not `['user']` or `['selector:user']`. But it also says "or local selector names" — for a
selector that reads `bookId` primitive, dep is `bookId` (which is both a local selector name and a root leaf).

So the rule: each selector's dependency set is a list of leaf paths/identifiers:
- Calling another selector A and using its full output (or if A returns primitive): dep = `A` (local selector name, which doubles as root leaf).
- Accessing property `x` of A's output: dep = `A.x` (if A is a root-level reducer selector) — this is a leaf path.
- Accessing nested property: `A.x.y`.
- For arrays: `A.0`.
- For maps: `A.map:key`.
- For sets: `A.set:value`.

And if A's output is itself derived (e.g., `upperCaseName` depends on `capitalizedName` which depends on `name`), then B depending on `upperCaseName`
might read `upperCaseName` directly (a string) so dep = `upperCaseName`. Good.

Then invalidation:
- When state changes, compute changed leaf paths relative to each logic root (e.g., `user.age`). Mark any selector whose deps match changed paths (d
== p or d prefix of p).
- When selector A re-evaluates, compute changed leaf paths between A's old and new output, **relative to A's name** (e.g., A=user, old/new diff gives
`user.age`). Then invalidate B if B's deps intersect A's changed leaves. But if A is a root reducer selector, its output change is already captured by
the state diff (since A reads from state). However if A is a derived selector (e.g., `upperCaseName`), state diff won't include `upperCaseName`. So we
need to propagate derived changes.

But we can simplify: After each action, we don't necessarily re-evaluate all selectors eagerly. Instead, we compute the state changed paths, mark
affected root-level selector outputs as dirty, and then when a selector is evaluated (on demand), if dirty, re-run; then diff old vs new output and
propagate to dependents lazily. React's useSyncExternalStore calls getSnapshot after dispatch, which invokes selectors on demand. So propagation can
happen during evaluation.

However, the requirement "Atomic Updates: Multiple dependency changes within a single action must trigger exactly one re-evaluation of a dependent
selector." This implies we shouldn't re-evaluate more than once per action. With lazy evaluation, if a selector is dirty and called multiple times
during the same action's notification, we should re-evaluate once and cache. If B depends on A and A is dirty, when B is evaluated it first evaluates
A (once) then B (once). If multiple changed paths affect B, B evaluates once. Good.

But there's a subtle issue: a selector's dependency set may change over time (e.g., `book` reads `books.1` first, then after `bookId` changes reads
`books.2`). When invalidating, we use the **last recorded** deps. That's fine.

Now the tricky part: to compute "changed leaf paths between A's old and new output" for derived selectors, and to record B's leaf deps into A's
output, we need proxies over selector outputs. Let's design the proxy.

**Tracking proxy design**

We'll create a `trackingProxy(value, basePath, tracker)` that returns a Proxy (for objects/arrays) or maybe wraps Map/Set too. When a property is
accessed:
- Record dependency `basePath + '.' + key` (or for Map: `basePath + '.map:' + key`; Set: `basePath + '.set:' + value`).
- Return a tracking proxy of the value at that path, so nested accesses record deeper paths.
- For arrays: accessing index `i` records `basePath + '.' + i`. Array methods like `.includes(value)` should record... The requirement: "using
advanced `Array` methods (e.g., `.includes()`), the dependency should reflect the specific key, membership, or elements checked." For
`.includes(value)` on an array, what dependency? It says "the dependency should reflect the specific ... elements checked." For Array.includes, we
might record each element checked? That seems impossible because `includes` compares all elements until found. The dependency should be the elements
checked — but that's all elements up to match. Maybe record the array as a whole? Hmm. Let's re-read: "When reading from a `Map` or `Set`, or using
advanced `Array` methods (e.g., `.includes()`), the dependency should reflect the specific key, membership, or elements checked. Dependency strings
use: for Map key access, `<reducer>.map:<key>` (e.g. `data.map:a`); for Set membership, `<reducer>.set:<value>` (e.g. `data.set:a`); for Array indices
read, `<reducer>.<index>` (e.g. `list.0`, `list.1`)."

So for `.includes()`, maybe dependency should reflect the elements checked — i.e., all array elements? Or the membership of the value? The spec only
defines strings for map/set/array indices. For array `.includes(value)`, the natural analog is `<reducer>.<index>` for each index compared. But the
phrase "the specific ... elements checked" suggests recording indices checked. Hmm, but `includes` is a method call; we can intercept `includes` on
the array proxy, and since the underlying method traverses, we could record all indices as dependencies (or the whole array). Simplest robust: for
`.includes(value)`, record dependencies on every index of the array (`list.0`, `list.1`, ...). Then if any element changes, the selector re-evaluates
(because membership could change). That satisfies "elements checked" in the sense that all elements are checked. But it may cause re-evaluation on
unrelated element changes — but `includes` genuinely depends on all elements (membership can change if any element changes). So recording all indices
is correct.

But how do we know the array indices? At proxy creation time we know the length, so we can record `0..length-1`. For `.includes` on a proxy of an
array, we can intercept method `includes` and record all indices. Also `indexOf`, `findIndex`, `find`, `some`, `every`, `map`, `filter`, etc. But the
requirement specifically mentions `.includes()`. We should at least handle `includes`. Other methods like `map` return a new array; the callback
accesses elements, which would be through the proxy? Actually if we return a proxy for `.map` result, the original elements accessed inside callback
would be native values, not tracked. But for this task, let's handle the mentioned cases: Map get/has, Set has, Array index read, Array includes.

Let's focus on what's testable:
- `user.name` vs `user.age` granularity.
- Map: `data.get('a')` -> dep `data.map:a`.
- Set: `data.has('a')` -> dep `data.set:a`.
- Array: `list[0]` -> dep `list.0`; `list.includes('x')` -> maybe `list.0...`.
- Multi-level selector chains propagation.
- Atomic updates.
- Circular safety.
- Health API.
- React integration.

We need to be careful: Kea's selectors typically read reducer selectors which return **immutable state values**. The proxies are transient—they exist
only while a selector is being evaluated. We return proxies to downstream selectors during evaluation. But after evaluation, the cached result stored
should be the **real value**, not the proxy (to avoid leaking proxies and to ensure Object.is comparisons work). Actually if we store the proxy as
cached result, then React's getSnapshot would return a proxy; but the proxy forwards all operations to the real object, so it works. But `Object.is`
between two different proxies of the same underlying value would be false, causing spurious re-renders. So we must store the real value as cached, and
only use proxies transiently while evaluating dependent selectors.

So flow:
- `atomicSelector` wrapper for name N:
  - If not dirty, return `cachedResult[N]` (real value).
  - If dirty (or first run):
    - Begin capture session (tracker = new DependencyTracker with base path = N? Actually the wrapper's base path for returned value is N).
    - Compute args: each arg is either a selector wrapper (which when called runs with its own tracking; but from the perspective of the caller, the
caller records a dependency on the callee's **leaf path within callee output** only when it accesses properties of the returned value). So the caller
should NOT record `selector:N` as a leaf dep directly; instead it records via property access on the returned proxy. But if callee returns a primitive
(e.g., `bookId`), the caller doesn't access any property; then we need to record the dependency as `bookId`. So when the caller invokes callee wrapper
and gets a primitive, the caller records dep `bookId`. If it gets an object, we should not yet record `bookId`; instead return a proxy that records
`bookId.prop` when accessed. But what if the caller uses the whole object (e.g., passes it to another function that doesn't access properties)? Then
no dependency is recorded at all, which would be wrong. But in practice a selector that takes a whole object as input and returns it unchanged, e.g.
`sameUser: [(s)=>[s.user], (user)=>user]`, would have no dependency. That's a problem: changing `user.name` should invalidate `sameUser` (its output
changes).

Hmm. So we need a strategy: record a dependency on the callee selector name at minimum, and then **refine** it when properties are accessed. In the
metadata, we could store a set of dependencies that includes both `user` and `user.name`. But then `user.age` change would invalidate via `user` even
though only `user.name` was read. Unless we *remove* the broad dep when a refined leaf dep is recorded. Let's think.

Approach: For each selector call from a parent selector, maintain a "current dependency" for that callee: initially `calleeName` (broad). When the
returned proxy is accessed at `.name`, we *replace* the broad dep with `calleeName.name`. If accessed at `.age` too, deps become `calleeName.name`,
`calleeName.age`. If no property access occurs, keep `calleeName`. This gives:
- `sameUser` (just returns user object): dep `user`. Any change under user -> re-evaluate (correct, because output object changes).
- `userName: [(s)=>[s.user], (user)=>user.name]`: dep `user.name` (after accessing .name). `user.age` change -> no re-eval. Correct.
- `book` reading `books[bookId]`: dep `books.1` + dep `bookId` (primitive, no refinement). When `bookId` changes -> re-eval via `bookId`; when
`books.2` changes -> no re-eval (dep books.1). Correct.

What about a selector that does `const name = user.name; const age = user.age;`? Deps `user.name`, `user.age`. Both tracked. Correct.

What about `user.name.length`? Dep `user.name.length`. If `user.name` changes to a different string but same length, the selector output (length)
doesn't change, but our dep `user.name.length` would be considered changed only if the length value changed. But the change path from state diff is
`user.name`, which doesn't match `user.name.length` by our prefix rule (d is prefix of p). Actually d=`user.name.length`, p=`user.name`: p is NOT
prefix of d; d is prefix of p. The rule was "change path p invalidates dep d if d===p or d is prefix of p". For p=`user.name`, d=`user.name.length`: d
is NOT prefix of p (it's longer). So we would NOT invalidate. But if the string's length changed, we need to know. The state diff only records
`user.name` (the string leaf changed). Since a string has no children, we could treat string/primitive leaf changes as changing any dep that is a
descendant of that leaf? But there are no descendant paths of a string. So `user.name.length` would never be invalidated. This is an edge case. We
could handle it by: when a selector accesses a property of a primitive (e.g., string `.length`), record dep `user.name` (the parent leaf) instead of
`user.name.length`. Actually for strings, `.length` is a property of the primitive; we can't proxy primitives easily. We can special-case: when
accessing a property on a primitive (string length), record the parent path. That's acceptable and semantically correct (any change to the string
invalidates). Let's do that.

Now, how to implement "replace broad dep when refined"? The tracker can keep a map from callee selector name -> set of leaf deps. When the caller
invokes callee, we record an entry `{selector: calleeName, paths: new Set()}` and mark a "no property accessed" state. When a property is accessed on
the returned proxy, we add `calleeName.prop...` to that entry's paths. At the end of the selector evaluation, for each callee entry:
- If paths is empty, add dep `calleeName` (broad).
- Else add all leaf paths, and don't add broad `calleeName`.
This means a selector that reads `user.name` has dep `user.name` (not `user`), which is exactly what we want.

But what about the dependents graph / topological order? For health, `dependents` should list local names of selectors that depend on this one. If B
depends on A via leaf `user.name`, then A's `dependents` should include B. We can determine that from B's recorded dependencies: for each dep that
starts with a selector name, the first segment is the dependency selector. But ambiguity: a dep `user.name` could be a state leaf `user.name` (if
`user` is a root reducer) or a property of selector `user`. Both are actually the same thing if `user` is a root reducer selector. If `user` is a
derived selector, then `user.name` is a property of its output. The first segment identifies the direct dependency selector name. So we can compute
dependents: for each selector B, for each dep, if dep starts with `knownSelectorName + '.'` or equals `knownSelectorName`, add B to that selector's
dependents. For map/set deps `data.map:a`, first segment is `data`. For array `list.0`, first segment `list`. Good.

But there's a catch: what about a dep like `books.1` where `1` is just a property key of an object (not a selector). The first segment `books` is a
selector name. Good. What about a dep that's a state leaf directly from the root proxy (not through a named selector)? E.g., a selector might read
`logic.selector(state).user.name` directly rather than through `selectors.user`. In Kea's API, user selectors access state via `s.user` (the selectors
proxy), not directly. But `func` could call `logic.selector`? Not typical. Reducer selectors read `logic.selector(state)` then `.key`. So root reads
happen in reducer selectors: `state.user` -> dep `user` with callee entry? Actually for a reducer selector `user`, the state root proxy is accessed
(not via a named selector), so its dep should be `user` (relative leaf). The "callee" is the root slice itself, not a selector. So we need a separate
concept: direct state deps (relative leaf paths) vs selector deps (via named selectors). Both end up as leaf paths in the dependency list. For a
reducer selector `user`, the dep is `user` (a leaf path at root). For a user selector `userName` reading `selectors.user` and `.name`, dep is
`user.name` (leaf path into selector `user` output, which maps to state leaf `user.name`). For `bookId` selector reading `selectors.bookId` primitive,
dep is `bookId` (leaf at root). So actually all deps can be represented as leaf paths from logic root. Great! The dependency list is just a list of
leaf paths.

Now invalidation:
- State changed paths are relative to logic root (computed by diffing the logic's slice: `logic.selector(oldState)` vs `logic.selector(newState)`).
Mark selectors dirty if any dep matches changed path.
- For derived selectors: when a selector A is evaluated and its output changes, diff old vs new output to compute changed leaf paths relative to A's
name. Then mark its dependents dirty if their deps match those changed paths. But do we need this eagerly? We can do it lazily: when a dependent B is
evaluated, we first ensure its dependencies (which include A's output leaves) are fresh. Actually B's evaluation calls A's wrapper, which returns A's
current value (after evaluating A if dirty). B then checks its own dirty flag. The dirty flag for B is set when:
  1. State changed paths match B's deps, OR
  2. A dependency selector A's **relevant leaves** changed.

How does B know that A's relevant leaves changed? When A re-evaluates (because it was dirty), it can diff old vs new output and mark dependents whose
deps match. But if A was not dirty (state didn't change its deps), then its output didn't change, so no need to mark dependents. So propagation:
- On state change, compute global changed paths for the logic slice. For each selector, if its deps match changed paths -> mark dirty with dirtyCause
= raw leaf path(s).
- When evaluating a dirty selector A, after recompute, diff A's old output vs new output, producing changed leaves relative to A's name. For each
dependent B, if B's deps match those changed leaves -> mark B dirty with dirtyCause = `selector:A`. But careful: if B is already dirty from state
change, keep the state cause? The requirement: "dirtyCause: The identifier that triggered the most recent invalidation." It should be one identifier.
Multiple changes in one action: e.g., both `user.name` and `user.age` change in one action, and B reads `user.name`; dirtyCause should be `user.name`
(a raw leaf path). If B is invalidated by selector A, cause `selector:A`. If B is already dirty from a state change, and then A also invalidates, keep
the first? Or most recent? The spec says "most recent invalidation" — with atomic updates, multiple changes in a single action should still trigger
exactly one re-evaluation. The dirtyCause could be any of the causes; likely the first or last. We'll define: when marking dirty, if already dirty,
keep the existing cause (the earliest). That seems reasonable.

Now, a big issue: how do we know when A's output changed leaf-wise? For a root reducer selector A (e.g., `user`), its output is `state.user`. State
diff already gives `user.name`, `user.age`. If A is dirty and re-evaluated, its old vs new output diff gives same leaves. So propagation from A to B
could be handled by the state diff alone: B's deps `user.name` match state change `user.name`, so B is marked dirty directly. We don't need to
propagate through A at all for root reducers. But for derived selectors, e.g., `capitalizedName` -> `upperCaseName`: state changes `name`. Root
reducer selector `name` is dirty (dep `name`). `capitalizedName` has dep `name` (it reads `selectors.name` primitive) so it's dirty directly.
`upperCaseName` has dep `capitalizedName` (primitive string) so it's dirty directly too! Wait: if `upperCaseName` reads `selectors.capitalizedName`
and uses the whole string, its dep is `capitalizedName`. State change `name` doesn't match `capitalizedName`. So we need to propagate: when
`capitalizedName` re-evaluates and output changes, mark `upperCaseName` dirty (via selector dep `capitalizedName`).

So propagation works via selector-level dependents when the dependent depends on the **whole output** of the selector (dep = selector name, no
refinement). For leaf-refined deps (e.g., `user.name`), the state diff should handle it because the underlying state leaf changed. But what about
derived selector A whose output is an object, and B reads `A.name`? E.g.:
- `userInfo` selector computes an object `{ name: ..., age: ... }` from various state.
- B reads `selectors.userInfo` and accesses `.name`.
- B's dep is `userInfo.name`.
- When `name` state changes, `userInfo` is dirty (dep includes `name`), re-evaluates, output object changes. State diff changed `name` only, which
doesn't match `userInfo.name`. So B wouldn't be marked dirty by state diff. We need to propagate from `userInfo` to B when `userInfo`'s `.name` leaf
changed. So when A re-evaluates, we diff old/new output and mark dependents whose deps match A's changed leaves. This is essential for derived object
selectors.

So the full propagation:
1. State change: compute changed leaf paths relative to logic root. For each selector, mark dirty if any dep matches (dep == p or dep prefix of p).
This directly handles root reducer selectors and selectors that read root state leaves through reducer selectors (since deps like `user.name` match
state change `user.name`).
2. When a selector A is evaluated (because dirty), compute old vs new output diff. For each dependent B, if B's deps match any of A's changed leaf
paths (with A's name as prefix), mark B dirty (cause `selector:A`). This handles derived object selectors and also could handle primitive derived
selectors (dep `A` matches changed leaf `A` itself? If A's output is primitive and changed, diff gives changed leaf path `A`; B's dep `A` matches).
3. If B is then evaluated, it will recompute.

But note: step 1 already marks B dirty for `userInfo.name`? No, because state change is `name`, and `userInfo.name` doesn't match `name`. So B relies
on step 2. But step 2 only runs when `userInfo` is evaluated. If `userInfo` is not dirty? It should be dirty because it depends on `name`. But will it
be evaluated? It's evaluated when someone calls it. When B is evaluated, B calls A (userInfo) as an input; A evaluates (dirty), then A propagates to
B. But B was already being evaluated... Let's think about ordering.

The evaluation of B:
- `atomicSelector('B', state, props)`:
  - Check if B dirty. It might not be marked dirty yet if propagation hasn't happened (e.g., React calls B directly after dispatch; B's dirty flag was
not set because propagation through A hasn't run).
  - If not dirty, it would return cached result, missing the update. So we can't rely solely on dirty flags propagated lazily. We need B to check
whether its dependencies have changed **at evaluation time**.

Better approach: B doesn't rely on a precomputed dirty flag; instead, at evaluation time, B checks each dependency:
- For each direct selector dependency (the callee selectors it calls as inputs), it evaluates them (ensuring they're up to date) and then checks
whether the **leaf portions it reads** changed. How? The callee wrappers, when called during B's evaluation, can indicate if their output changed (or
if the relevant leaves changed). If A's output changed in a way that affects B's deps, B re-evaluates.
- For state leaf deps (which are tracked as leaf paths under root reducer selectors), the state diff at dispatch time can mark them dirty. But B's
deps are leaf paths like `user.name`; when B is called, it can also just compare the actual current leaf value against the value it used last time?
That's complex.

Simplest robust architecture: **Eager invalidation on every action**, including propagation through the dependency graph, but done by evaluating all
affected selectors? That would violate "propagation only to affected selectors" and could be expensive. But the requirement says "If a selector's
inputs haven't changed, it should not re-evaluate." Eagerly evaluating all would violate that if we evaluate unaffected selectors. However we can
propagate lazily by maintaining, for each selector, a "dependency versions" mechanism.

Let me think of a version-based invalidation:
- Each leaf path in the state has a version (global counter incremented on each action if that leaf changed). Actually we can store a `version` for
each path.
- Each selector records, for each dependency, the version of that dependency at the time it was computed.
- On evaluation, for each dep, look up current version and compare to recorded version. If any differs, selector is dirty.
- For selector deps (e.g., `capitalizedName`), the "version" of that dependency is the version of the underlying state leaves that feed it? But if
`capitalizedName` is derived from `name`, then its "version" should change when `name` changes. We could assign each selector a `computedVersion` that
increments each time its output recomputes (or more precisely, when its relevant output changes). Then B stores `capitalizedNameVersion`; if changed,
B recomputes.

But if B depends on `user.name` (leaf within A's output), and A recomputes due to `user.age` changing (but `.name` same), B should NOT recompute. With
per-selector computedVersion, B would see A's version increment and recompute — wrong. So we need per-leaf versions within selector outputs.

Alternative: store in each dependency the **value** of the leaf at last compute, and compare on evaluation. For state leaves: when B computes, it
reads actual state values (via the underlying real state) for its deps and compares with stored. For selector leaf deps: when B computes, it can ask A
for the leaf value. But comparing arbitrary values each time may be expensive but correct. Actually simpler: for each dep path, we can store a
"version" that is incremented whenever that specific path changes. For state paths, increment version on dispatch diff. For selector-output paths,
when A recomputes, we diff A's old/new output and increment versions for the changed leaf paths (relative to A's name). Then B compares dep versions.

Let's define a global per-context (or per-logic) `versions: Map<string, number>` where keys are full relative paths within a logic slice, e.g.
`user.name`, `user`, `capitalizedName`. Actually we can use one map per logic to avoid collisions. On each dispatch:
1. Diff state slice old vs new -> set of changed leaf paths `P` (relative to logic root).
2. For each path p in P, increment `versions[p]`.
3. Also mark all selectors dirty if any dep matches p (dep == p or dep prefix of p). But maybe we don't even need dirty flags; version comparison at
eval time suffices. But for dirtyCause and evaluation counting, we can compute at eval time.

When selector A recomputes:
1. Diff old output vs new output relative to A's name -> changed leaf paths `Q` (e.g., `A.name`, `A.age`).
2. For each q in Q, increment `versions[q]`.
3. This will cause dependents to detect version changes when they evaluate.

At selector evaluation time:
- For each dep `d`, get current version `versions.get(d)` (or 0 if undefined) and compare to `lastVersions.get(d)` stored. If any differ, selector is
dirty.
- But wait: for dep `user.name`, version increments on state change `user.name`; so B detects dirty when evaluated. For dep `userInfo.name` where
userInfo is derived, version increments when A recomputes (via diff). So B detects. Great — no need for dirty flags/pre-propagation; evaluation-time
version check works.
- However, there's a problem: if B's dep is `capitalizedName` (whole derived selector output), and `capitalizedName` recomputes but produces the
**same string** (e.g., name changes case but same normalized value), the diff old vs new output would produce no changed leaves (since values equal),
so version doesn't increment and B won't recompute. That's correct: B's input didn't change.
- If A recomputes but output object is new with same leaf values, diff yields no changed leaves -> no propagation. Correct.

This version-based lazy approach seems elegant. But we need to ensure `lastVersions` are stored per selector and that B records its deps correctly
each time (since deps may change). Also for **state** dep paths, we need versions incremented on state diff. For a dep like `user` (whole object),
version increments on `user.name` change? We said a change at `user.name` should invalidate a dep `user` (since d is prefix of p). So at eval time,
for dep `d`, we should check not just `versions.get(d)` but whether any changed path under `d` occurred. We can store a global `changeLog` (list of
changed paths per dispatch or per recompute) and at eval time check if any change path matches dep. But versions per exact path don't capture
"descendant changed". E.g., dep `user`, changes `user.name` -> we need to know. We could increment a "descendant version" for every ancestor path too.
When a leaf path changes, increment versions for that path AND all its ancestors (prefixes). So `user.name` change increments `user.name`, `user`, and
maybe root. Then dep `user` detects. Dep `user.name` detects. Dep `user.name.length` (not a real path) doesn't. Good. That's simple: when diffing
changed leaves, for each changed leaf path, increment version at that path and all ancestor paths. But doing this for every dispatch with deep paths
is fine.

Similarly when A recomputes and changed leaf `A.name` occurs, increment versions for `A.name` and ancestors `A`. So dep `A` (whole selector) detects
when any leaf in A's output changes. Dep `A.name` detects when that leaf changes.

Now, there's a subtlety with maps/sets: changed path `data.map:a` and its ancestor `data`. We'll treat `map:a` as a leaf component; the ancestor path
for `data.map:a` is `data`. For prefix matching, `data` is prefix. Good.

Now, how do we compute changed leaf paths from state diff? Let's write a `diffPaths(oldVal, newVal, base)`:
- If oldVal === newVal, return [].
- If both are Map: compare sizes and each key. For each key, if old.has(k) !== new.has(k), record `${base}.map:${k}`. If both have k and old.get(k)
!== new.get(k), record `${base}.map:${k}` (and recurse? Map values are leaves; if map value is object, should we recurse? The dependency for map
access is at the key level — "Map key access, `<reducer>.map:<key>`". So even if the value object changes internally, the dep is still `map:key`. We
can just record `map:key` without recursing into value. That's fine-grained enough at the map level.)
- If both are Set: compare membership; for each value present in one but not both, record `${base}.set:${value}`. For values in both, if the value
object changes? Set membership is identity-based, so values are either present or not; no recursion.
- If both are Array: compare length; if length differs, record indices beyond min length? For each index i in 0..max-1: if old[i] !== new[i], record
`${base}.${i}` and recurse into element if both objects. Also if length changed, we could record changed indices plus maybe the whole array? The deps
are indices read; if array grows, element at new index is "changed". We'll record indices where values differ.
- If both are plain objects: for union of keys, if old[k] !== new[k], record `${base}.${k}` and recurse into value if both are
objects/arrays/maps/sets (to get deeper leaves). Actually for granularity we need to recurse: if `user` object changes, record `user.name` not just
`user`. So recurse for object/array values. But for primitive values, record the leaf.
- Otherwise (different primitives or types): record `${base}`.

One concern: if a nested object is replaced with a completely different object and some keys removed, recursion still records leaf changes for keys
present in both or added. For removed keys, record `${base}.${k}`. Good.

Now, what about state diff for the **whole store** vs logic slice? We need changed paths relative to each logic's root. We could diff the whole store
once and then for each logic map to local. But simpler: each logic's engine, on dispatch, diffs `logic.selector(oldState)` vs
`logic.selector(newState)` (the logic slice). That's O(slice size) per logic, which could be expensive with many logics. But acceptable for tests. To
optimize, we can diff whole store once and distribute paths. But implementing mapping is a bit more code. Let's consider: each logic's root is at
`logic.path` (e.g. `['scenes','something']`). The global state diff could produce global paths like `scenes.something.user.name`. For each logic,
strip the prefix. But also there could be multiple logic at different paths. Let's implement a global diff in the context and then have each logic
engine subscribe to the store and compute its own slice diff. Simpler to keep per-logic diff for correctness. But the state can be huge and this runs
on every dispatch for every mounted logic. For this task, acceptable.

Actually, we can hook into Redux via middleware or store.subscribe. The store's subscribe callback receives no args; we need previous state. We can
create a middleware that captures `getState()` before/after and calls `engine.notify(prevState, nextState)`. Or we can modify the store subscription.
Since `useSelector` uses `store.subscribe`, and the engine also needs to update versions before React selectors run, the order matters: engine must
process the new state before React's getSnapshot calls. Middleware runs during dispatch, before subscribers. So a middleware is ideal: after
`next(action)`, get new state, call engine notify. That way by the time subscribers run, versions are updated.

But there may be multiple logics. We can have a per-context registry of atomic engines, and a middleware installed when `atomicSelectors` is enabled.
Or simpler: each engine can subscribe to the store and handle batching. But middleware is cleaner.

Wait — there's also the `batchChanges` mechanism that dispatches `@KEA/FLUSH` after a batch. With atomic selectors, the engine should probably process
after the actual state-changing action, and React's subscription is paused during mount. But engine processing during batch could still work. The key:
versions updated before React getSnapshot. Middleware does that.

However, the task says "The new engine intercepts core lifecycle hooks; valid implementations must ensure that standard plugin event ordering (e.g.,
afterMount) is not disrupted." This hints they expect the engine implemented as a **plugin** that hooks into `beforeMount`/`afterMount` etc. Let's
think about implementing as a plugin. But it could also be built into core selectors. The mention "intercepts core lifecycle hooks" suggests we should
add an `atomicSelectors` plugin that hooks `afterMount` to set up subscriptions and `beforeUnmount` to tear down. But we also need to modify selector
creation.

Let's design as a plugin or integrated? Given the requirement to make `logic.selectorHealth()` available and to track deps, and to preserve lifecycle
ordering, we can implement:
- Add `atomicSelectors` option to context.
- In `src/core/selectors.ts` and `src/core/reducers.ts`, when context option atomicSelectors is true, use an atomic selector factory instead of
reselect.
- Add engine setup in build/mount lifecycle.

Let me think about where to put the engine. Could create `src/core/atomic-selectors.ts` with the engine.

Let's define the core data structures:

```ts
interface AtomicSelectorMeta {
  name: string
  compute: (...args:any[]) => any
  args: Selector[]
  deps: Set<string>          // relative leaf path dependencies
  dependents: Set<string>
  lastDeps: Map<string, number> // dep -> version at last compute
  cached: any
  dirty: boolean
  dirtyCause: string | null
  evaluations: number
}
```

But as argued, we can use version comparison instead of dirty flags. Still need a `dirty` flag for dirtyCause and to know whether to recompute.
Actually dirtyCause should reflect the identifier that triggered most recent invalidation. We can compute at evaluation time: if any dep version
changed, set dirtyCause to the first changed dep identifier and return dirty. For selector-caused invalidation, the dep would be like
`capitalizedName` (a selector name) — but wait, if B's dep is `capitalizedName`, and version of `capitalizedName` changed (because capitalName
recomputed with different output), at eval time B sees version changed and dirtyCause would be `capitalizedName`? The spec says use
`selector:capitalizedName`. So we need to distinguish selector deps from state leaf deps. In the metadata we can store both `deps` (leaf paths, e.g.
`user.name`, `capitalizedName`) and `selectorDeps` (e.g. `capitalizedName`). When B's dep `capitalizedName` changes, dirtyCause should be
`selector:capitalizedName`. When dep `user.name` changes, dirtyCause `user.name`.

But `capitalizedName` as a leaf path (the whole selector output) is also a state leaf if `capitalizedName` is a root reducer. We can determine: if the
first segment of the dep matches a known selector name AND that selector is derived (not a root reducer selector?), hmm. Actually for a root reducer
selector `user`, if B reads `user.name`, dep is `user.name` and state change cause should be `user.name`. If B reads whole `user`, dep is `user` and
state change cause should be `user` (raw leaf path). If B reads whole `capitalizedName` (derived), cause should be `selector:capitalizedName`. How to
distinguish? We can record, for each dep, whether it came from a selector call (and if the whole output was used without refinement, mark it as
selector dep) or from direct state read (reducer selector root read).

Let's refine the tracker:
- For each selector evaluation, the tracker has a root tracking state (the logic slice proxy) used by reducer selectors to record direct state deps.
When a reducer selector reads `state.user`, it records a direct state dep `user` (leaf path).
- When a user selector B calls another selector A via the `s.user` proxy:
  - It records a "selector dep" on A, initially whole-output (`A`), refined by property access to `A.prop...`.
  - In the final deps list, a whole-output selector dep is stored as `A` but marked as selectorDep. When A's output changes (version of `A` increments
because a leaf under A changed), dirtyCause is `selector:A`. If refined to `A.prop`, store as `A.prop` and treat as state leaf dep (cause `A.prop`).

But for a root reducer selector A, when B reads whole A output (e.g., `books` object used wholesale), the dep `books` is also a state leaf. If
`books.1` changes, version of `books` increments (ancestor), so B would be invalidated with cause... should it be `selector:books` or `books`? Since B
used the whole books object, a change to any book invalidates B; cause could be `books` (raw leaf) or `selector:books`. Spec says
`selector:<localName>` when caused by another selector. B was caused by `books` selector output changing. Either is arguably fine. To keep it simple
and match "local selector names" in dependencies, I'd say for whole-output selector deps, use `selector:<name>` as cause and list `name` in
dependencies. For refined leaf deps, use raw path as cause and list path in dependencies.

So metadata per selector:
- `dependencies: Set<string>` — leaf paths and local selector names (as displayed).
- `dependents: Set<string>`
- `evaluations`
- `dirtyCause`
- `lastDepVersions: Map<string, number>`
- `depKinds: Map<string, 'selector'|'state'>` — to determine cause formatting. For dependency `books`, kind selector. For `user.name`, kind state.
- `compute`, `args`, `cached`, `versions` internal.

Wait, but for `user.name`, if `user` is a root reducer selector, B's dep `user.name` is kind state. For a derived selector `userInfo`, B's dep
`userInfo.name` is also kind state (raw path cause `userInfo.name`) per spec example? The spec says dependencies are relative paths like `user.name`;
dirtyCause raw leaf paths e.g. `user.name` when caused by state change. It doesn't mention derived selector property causes. So `userInfo.name` as raw
path is fine.

Now versions:
- Global per-logic `versions: Map<string, number>`.
- On state diff: for each changed leaf path p (relative to logic root), increment version for p and all ancestors. But for deps like `user.name`,
version at `user.name` increments. For dep `user`, version at `user` increments (ancestor). Good.
- On selector A recompute: diff old vs new output relative to A name -> changed leaf paths q (e.g., `A.name`, `A.age`). Increment version for q and
ancestors. For dep `A` (whole selector), version at `A` increments if any leaf changed. For dep `A.name`, version at `A.name` increments if that leaf
changed. Good.
- At selector B eval: for each dep d in B.deps, check `versions.get(d)` vs B.lastDepVersions.get(d). If differs -> dirty. Determine cause based on
depKind. If multiple changed, pick first (insertion order).

Now, to compute changed leaves of A's output relative to A name: we call `diffPaths(oldVal, newVal, A_name)`. If A output is primitive and changed,
diff returns `[A_name]`? Our `diffPaths` for non-objects with old!==new returns `[base]`. Good.

Now the tracker proxy. Let's implement a class `DependencyTracker` with:
- `stateRoot: any` — the logic slice (real value) wrapped in a proxy for reducer selectors.
- `versions: Map`, `depsBySelector` etc.
- For a given evaluation, we need to collect deps. But we need to distinguish deps recorded by the **currently evaluated selector** from deps recorded
by callee selectors. When B evaluates and calls A, A's own evaluation will use its own tracker session (so A's deps are recorded separately). B should
only record the call to A and property accesses on A's returned value. So each selector evaluation has its own tracker session with:
  - `rootProxy`: proxy for direct state reads (used by reducer selectors).
  - `selectorProxy`: proxy around `logic.selectors` to intercept calls to named selectors and return tracking-wrapped outputs.
  - `pendingSelectorDeps: Map<name, {paths: Set<string>, wholeUsed: boolean}>` — for calls made by this selector to other selectors.
  - `stateDeps: Set<string>` — direct state leaf deps.

But wait: reducer selectors read the state root via `logic.selector(state)`. The atomic wrapper for reducer selector `user` should run with a session
whose rootProxy is the logic slice, and it records `stateDeps` = `['user']`. It doesn't call other selectors. So the wrapper can create a new session
for each selector evaluation. But when B's evaluation calls A, A's wrapper creates its own session, evaluates, and returns a **tracked proxy** that,
when accessed by B, records into B's session `pendingSelectorDeps[A]`. How to return a proxy that B can access? A's wrapper returns its cached real
value; but to record B's property accesses, B needs a proxy. So when B calls A's wrapper, the wrapper should return a proxy tied to B's session, not
the raw value. But A's wrapper doesn't know B's session unless passed via context.

Simplest: The `logic.selectors` proxy used by the **caller** returns a function that, when called, runs the callee with the current session context
and returns a tracking proxy registered in the caller's session. So:
- There is a global "current session" stack.
- When B evaluates, push B's session as current.
- B's `input` function calls `s.user` which is a proxy getter on the selectors object. It returns a function `(state, props) => callSelector(name,
state, props)`.
- When B's input calls that function, `callSelector`:
  1. Gets current session (B's session).
  2. Runs the callee A's wrapper. But A's wrapper needs its own session. So we set current session to A's session, run A, then restore B's session.
  3. After A computes, record in B's session: `pendingSelectorDeps[A].wholeUsed = true` initially.
  4. Return a tracking proxy `makeProxy(A.cached, A.name, B.session)` so that property access on it records refined deps into B's session.

But careful: A's wrapper when called by B should not re-evaluate if A is not dirty. It returns cached real value. We then wrap that real value in B's
tracking proxy. If B accesses `user.name`, B's session records `user.name` and `wholeUsed = false` (refined). Good.

Now for the input function itself: `input(logic.selectors, propSelectors)`. `logic.selectors` is the object of selector wrappers. We can create a
proxy for `logic.selectors` that returns these call functions. When B's input function runs, it calls `s.user` and `s.bookId`, which return functions;
those functions are passed in `args`. Later, when we evaluate args, we call each with `(state, props)`, triggering `callSelector`. Good.

But there's a subtlety: `args` may include prop selectors (plain functions) and selectors (the wrapper functions). We evaluate all args in order. When
we evaluate a selector arg, it records pending dep. When we evaluate a prop selector, it may read `props` (not tracked). Then we call compute
`func(...argResults)`. During func, property accesses on arg results (which are tracking proxies for selector outputs, or real prop values) record
refined deps. For selector outputs that are primitives, no proxy needed; the pending dep stays wholeUsed.

But the compute func might also access properties on the result of a prop selector — not tracked (props are not state).

Now, what about the `books` object: B's `book` selector calls `s.books` -> returns a proxy of books object in B's session. Then func does
`books[bookId]` -> proxy getter records `books.1` (pending dep paths for books). Great.

Now for the reducer selector itself (`books`): its compute is `(state) => state['books']` (created in reducers.ts via `createSelector(logic.selector,
(state) => state[key])`). In atomic mode, we need to create it differently. We can create an atomic selector where `args = [logic.selector]` and `func
= (root) => root[key]`. When evaluated, it uses its session rootProxy; `logic.selector(state)` returns the rootProxy (not raw), and `.books` access
records `stateDeps: ['books']`; returns the books value. Then its cached real value is stored (unwrap proxy at end). Good.

So the atomic selector factory takes `args` (array of functions) and `func`, and returns a wrapper function `(state, props) => value`. It needs to
know the logic and name.

Let's define the engine:

```ts
class AtomicSelectorEngine {
  logic: BuiltLogic
  versions = new Map<string, number>()
  selectors = new Map<string, AtomicSelector>()
  currentSession: Session | null = null

  createSelector(name, args, func): Selector {
    const meta = { name, args, func, deps: new Set(), dependents: new Set(), evaluations: 0, dirtyCause: null, cached: undefined, hasCached: false,
lastDepVersions: new Map(), depKinds: new Map(), ... }
    this.selectors.set(name, meta)
    const wrapper = (state, props) => this.run(name, state, props)
    return wrapper
  }

  run(name, state, props) {
    const meta = this.selectors.get(name)
    // determine dirty via versions
    const dirty = this.isDirty(meta)
    if (!dirty && meta.hasCached) {
      return meta.cached
    }
    // create session
    const session = new Session(this, meta)
    const prev = this.currentSession
    this.currentSession = session
    try {
      // root proxy: wraps logic.selector(state) real value
      const root = this.logic.selector(state)  // but logic.selector may itself be atomic? No, root selector is plain pathSelector
      // Actually to avoid double-wrapping, root should be raw. But logic.selector might be the root selector created by rootSelector().
      session.rootProxy = makeProxy(root, '', session, true) // direct state deps with base ''
      const argResults = meta.args.map(arg => arg(session.rootProxy, props))  // hmm args expect state not rootProxy
      ...
    }
  }
}
```

Wait, in Kea, the selector wrapper signature is `(state, props) => ...` where `state` is the full Redux state. The reducer selector's args include
`logic.selector` which expects full state. So when running a reducer selector, we need to call `logic.selector(state)` which returns the root slice.
We can't just replace the state arg with rootProxy; the arg functions expect full state.

Let me restructure: The atomic selector wrapper is `(state, props) => run(name, state, props)`. Inside `run`:
- Determine if dirty.
- Create session with rootProxy = makeProxy(logic.selector(state), '', session, true).
- For each arg function:
  - If arg is one of our selector wrappers (identify by a marker property `_atomicSelectorName`), call it with (state, props) as usual — it will run
its own evaluation. Record pending dep in session.
  - Else call arg(session.rootProxy, props)? Actually prop selectors are `(state, props) => props.extra`; they ignore state. But the generic selector
funcs (like `logic.selector`) need full state. Hmm.

Let's look at how reducer selector is built in current code:
